Title: Personalized Federated Learning for Tensor Regression

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.27191v1

Published Time: Fri, 28 Aug 2026 01:27:54 GMT

Number of Pages: 88

Markdown Content:
# Personalized Federated Learning for Tensor Regression 

## Kejun Chen, Xianqi Wei and Qianqian Zhu ∗

## Shanghai University of Finance and Economics 

## August 28, 2026 

Abstract 

The growing availability of tensor-valued data across multiple institutions creates opportunities for col-laborative analysis, but also raises challenges related to data privacy, high dimensionality, and client hetero-geneity. This paper introduces a personalized federated tensor regression framework that addresses all three simultaneously. Each client’s coefficient tensor is decomposed into a globally shared low-Tucker-rank com-ponent and a locally sparse deviation, estimated via a two-stage privacy-preserving procedure. We establish finite-sample upper bounds and minimax lower bounds that quantify the privacy-accuracy trade-off, and prove the consistency of the supporting initialization and rank-selection steps. Simulation studies confirm that the federated approach improves estimation and prediction over purely local methods, especially when per-client data are scarce, and an MRI-based ADHD study illustrates its strong performance under real privacy constraints. 

Keywords : tensor regression; federated learning; differential privacy; high-dimensional estimation; non-asymptotic theory. 

> ∗

Address for correspondence: Qianqian Zhu, School of Statistics and Data Science, Institute of Big Data Research, Shanghai University of Finance and Economics, Shanghai, China. Email: zhu.qianqian@mail.shufe.edu.cn 

1arXiv:2608.27191v1 [stat.ME] 27 Aug 2026 1 Introduction 

Recent advances in data collection and scientific measurement have made rich and high-dimensional datasets increasingly common in modern applications. In many fields, such as neuroimaging and medical imaging, observations are naturally recorded as multiway arrays indexed by spatial locations, imaging modalities, or other structured features. These tensor-valued data contain useful information for scientific discovery and clinical decision-making. For example, imaging data can be used to predict disease severity or other clinical outcomes (Hou and Chaib-draa, 2015; Guhaniyogi et al., 2017). Tensor regression provides a natural framework for modeling the relationship between tensor-valued covariates and responses. Tensor-valued data contain rich multiway information that is typically lost if the data are vectorized for standard regression analysis (Seber and Lee, 2003; Negahban et al., 2009). To fully exploit this structure, tensor regression directly models the relationship between the tensor-valued response and predictors, leading to several well-studied sub-frameworks: scalar-on-tensor regression (Zhou et al., 2013; Li et al., 2018), vector-on-tensor regression (Ding and Dennis Cook, 2018; Pfeiffer et al., 2021), and tensor-on-tensor regression (Lock, 2018; Luo and Zhang, 2024). However, the high dimensionality of tensor data makes unconstrained estimation impractical. Consequently, a central theme in the literature is to impose low-dimensional structural assumptions on the coefficient tensor, with the most common choices being low-rankness, sparsity, or a combination of both (Li and Zhang, 2017; Sun and Li, 2017; Raskutti et al., 2019). In practice, however, tensor-valued data are often collected across multiple sites, and the sample size available at each individual site can be limited. In the federated learning framework, we regard each participating site as a client. For instance, a single hospital or imaging center may only have a small number of subjects with available medical images. When the tensor dimension is large relative to the local sample size, relying solely on site-specific data may be insufficient for stable estimation and reliable prediction. A natural remedy is to borrow information across related sites. Federated learning provides a useful framework for this purpose by allowing clients to collaboratively estimate a model while retaining their raw data locally (Koneˇ cn´ y et al., 2015; McMahan et al., 2017). In a standard federated procedure, each client computes model parameters or gradients from its local data and communicates these updates to a central server, which aggregates them to update the global model (Kairouz and McMahan, 2021). Although federated learning avoids direct raw-data pooling, the communicated model updates may still re-veal sensitive information about local records. Decentralization alone therefore does not provide a formal privacy guarantee (Geyer et al., 2017; Kaissis et al., 2020). Several mechanisms have been developed to protect infor-mation during distributed computation, including secure multiparty computation (Yao, 1982), homomorphic encryption (Gentry, 2009), and secure aggregation (Bonawitz et al., 2017). These cryptographic mechanisms protect local inputs or client updates during computation and aggregation, but generally do not limit the infor-mation that may be revealed by the final model or other released outputs. Differential privacy (DP) provides a complementary form of protection by formally and quantitatively limiting the influence of any individual record on released results (Dwork et al., 2006; Dwork and Roth, 2014). In this paper, we adopt differential privacy by adding carefully calibrated random noise to the communicated updates during federated learning (Abadi et al., 2016; Dong et al., 2022). Beyond privacy concerns, multi-site tensor data are often highly heterogeneous. Differences in scanners, acquisition protocols, populations, or measurement procedures can introduce systematic site effects into imaging variables and their relationships with clinical outcomes (Fortin et al., 2018; Yamashita et al., 2019). Because the 2coefficient tensor captures this association, such site effects naturally lead to client-specific coefficient tensors, making a single shared tensor overly restrictive in federated settings (Smith et al., 2017; Li et al., 2020). Recent studies have extended scalar-on-tensor and tensor-on-tensor regression to federated settings (Konyar and Reisi Gahrooei, 2024; Zhang et al., 2024). These methods facilitate decentralized tensor analysis under data-sharing constraints, but they do not jointly provide formal privacy protection, heterogeneity accommodation, and theoretical guarantees. To address these gaps, we develop a personalized federated learning framework for tensor regression that integrates formal differential privacy, personalized modeling of client heterogeneity, and finite-sample theoretical guarantees within a unified approach. Our main contributions are threefold. (i) We introduce a personalized federated framework for tensor regression that simultaneously addresses high-dimensionality, differential privacy and client heterogeneity. By decomposing each coefficient tensor into a shared low Tucker-rank component and a client-specific weakly sparse component, the framework captures common low-dimensional structure across clients while preserving local deviations. This construction pro-vides a flexible and general approach for modeling tensor-valued responses and/or predictors in distributed environments. (ii) We develop two-stage estimation algorithms for both the federated setting and a single-client benchmark. In the federated regime, the shared component is first learned via differentially private gradient updates; subsequently, each client refines its own sparse deviation locally. The single-client procedure follows an analogous two-stage scheme without privacy protection. To enable stable implementation, we further provide an initialization estimator and a Tucker-rank selection strategy. (iii) We establish theoretical guarantees for both federated and single-client settings. For the federated pro-cedure, we derive non-asymptotic upper bounds and matching minimax lower bounds, confirming rate optimality. Parallel upper and lower bounds are provided for the single-client case, allowing us to char-acterize when the statistical benefit of cross-client information sharing outweighs the additional cost of privacy protection. In addition, we establish convergence of the initialization step and consistency of the rank selection criterion. The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section 2 introduces notation, tensor algebra, and the model setup. Section 3 presents the proposed two-stage estimation procedures for federated and single-client learning. Section 4 discusses implementation issues, including initialization, Tucker-rank selection, and tuning-parameter selection. Section 5 provides theoretical analysis for the proposed estimators. Sections 6 and 7 contain simulation studies and an empirical application, respectively. Additional notation, algorithmic details, implementation procedures, auxiliary theoretical results, and proofs are provided in the Supplementary Material. The dataset and computer programs for the analysis in Section 7 are available at https://github.com/weishenqi/PFTR .

# 2 Preliminaries and Problem Setup 

## 2.1 Notation and Tensor Algebra 

Vectors and matrices are denoted by boldface lowercase and uppercase letters, respectively (e.g., a, ξ, A, ∆). For a matrix A ∈ Rm×n, denote its transpose, Frobenius norm, and nuclear norm by A⊤, ∥A∥F, and ∥A∥∗,respectively. For ν ∈ [1 , ∞), the ℓν norm of A is defined as ∥A∥ν = ( Pmi=1 

Pnj=1 |Aij |ν )1/ν ; the same formula for ν ∈ (0 , 1) defines the entrywise ℓν quasi-norm. When ν = 0, ∥A∥0 = # {(i, j ) : Aij ̸ = 0 } is the number of 3nonzero entries of A. For sequences {xn} and {yn}, we write xn ≳ yn if there exists a constant C > 0 such that xn ≥ Cy n for all n, and xn ≍ yn if both xn ≳ yn and yn ≳ xn hold. For any positive integer n, let [n] = {1, 2, . . . , n }.For mode-ℓ tensor M ∈ Rp1×p2×···× pℓ and S ⊂ [ℓ], its multi-mode matricization M[S] is a Q

For any tensor M, its Frobenius norm is defined as ∥M∥F = p⟨M, M⟩. For any ν ∈ [0 , ∞), the entrywise ℓν norm (or quasi-norm when ν ∈ [0 , 1)) of M is identical to the ℓν norm of any matricization of M. That is, ∥M∥ν = ∥M[S]∥ν for any nonempty subset S ⊂ [ℓ]. 

## 2.2 Model Settings 

Suppose there are K clients, indexed by k ∈ [K]. Client k holds a local dataset Dk = {(Xk,i , Yk,i )}nk 

> i=1

with sample size nk, where Xk,i ∈ Rp1×···× pd is a mode-d covariate tensor and Yk,i ∈ Rq1×···× qm is a mode-m response tensor. For each fixed client k ∈ [K], we assume that ( Xk,i , Yk,i ) are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d. ) realizations generated from the following client-specific tensor regression model: 

Yk,i = Ak, Xk,i + Ek,i , k ∈ [K], i ∈ [nk], (1) where Ak ∈ Rq1×···× qm×p1×···× pd is the unknown coefficient tensor for client k, and Ek,i ∈ Rq1×···× qm is a mean-zero random noise tensor, which is i.i.d. across indices i. Furthermore, the noise sequences {Ek,i }nk 

> i=1

are assumed to be independent across different clients k ∈ [K]. To characterize the structural similarity among heterogeneous clients, we assume that each coefficient tensor 

Ak can be decomposed as follows: 

Ak = A0 + Bk, k ∈ [K], (2) where A0 is a global component shared by all clients, and Bk is a client-specific deviation. To address the high dimensionality of Ak, we further assume that A0 admits a low Tucker-rank structure and each Bk is sparse. It is worth noting that A0 and Bk are not uniquely identifiable. To alleviate this issue, we introduce the weak identifiability condition in Assumption 1 to ensure the decomposition remains meaningful without requiring strict uniqueness. The model in (1) defines a unified regression framework whose specific form is determined by the mode dimensions of the predictor and response tensors. Assuming the nondegenerate setting where pj > 1 for all 4j ∈ [d] and qj > 1 for all j ∈ [m], the framework subsumes several classical regression models. In particular, it reduces to regression with multivariate responses and covariates (Seber and Lee, 2003; Montgomery et al., 2021) when ( d, m ) = (1 , 1), to regression with multivariate responses and matrix-valued covariates (Ding and Dennis Cook, 2018; Pfeiffer et al., 2021) when ( d, m ) = (2 , 1), and to matrix-on-matrix regression (Lock, 2018) when ( d, m ) = (2 , 2). The framework also naturally accommodates scalar response scenarios. Specifically, it includes scalar-on-vector regression, which corresponds to the ordinary linear model (Seber and Lee, 2003; Montgomery et al., 2021); scalar-on-matrix regression, which reduces to trace regression and is fundamental to low-rank matrix estimation and matrix completion (Koltchinskii et al., 2011; Negahban and Wainwright, 2012); and higher-order scalar-on-tensor regression, a model widely adopted in neuroimaging and medical imaging studies (Zhou et al., 2013; Li et al., 2018). 

# 3 Two-Stage Estimation Procedure 

To fit model (1) with the decomposition (2), we proceed in two stages. First, we estimate the shared low Tucker-rank tensor A0 as a global representation. Given this shared component, we then estimate the sparse deviation tensor Bk for each client and obtain an estimate for the personalized coefficient tensor Ak. Sections 3.1 and 3.2 describe how this two-stage strategy is tailored to federated and single-client settings, respectively. 

## 3.1 Federated Learning 

For federated learning, Stage-I learns the shared component via differentially private federated procedure, whereas Stage-II performs local refinement using the shared estimate from Stage-I. 

Stage-I: Differentially Private Representation Learning. To separate the shared component A0 from client-specific deviations Bk in the decomposition (2), we temporarily treat Bk as nuisance parameters. Under (2), the original model (1) can be rewritten as

## 3.2 Single-Client Learning 

As a benchmark, we also consider a single-client learning strategy that relies solely on local data. In this setting, we adopt the same two-stage procedure, with the key differences that all computations are performed locally and no privacy-preserving mechanisms are required. 

Stage-I: Local Representation Learning. For a fixed client k ∈ [K], Stage-I estimates the shared low Tucker-rank component using only its own dataset Dk. As in the federated setting, treating Bk in (2) as a nuisance parameter leads to the local least-squares loss ℓk(A) = (2 nk)−1 Pnk

# 4 Implementary Issues 

## 4.1 Algorithm Initialization 

Both the federated and single-client two-stage procedures require a suitable initial value for the low Tucker-rank component A0. We begin by constructing a preliminary one-stage estimator for each client using its local dataset. Recall that each Ak in (2) decomposes into a low Tucker-rank shared part and a weakly sparse deviation. A natural approach is to estimate both components jointly via a penalized least-squares formulation. While the sum of mode-wise nuclear norms, Pd+ms=1 ∥A[s]∥∗, is a classical convex surrogate for the Tucker rank, it is known to perform poorly in recovering the underlying Tucker-rank structure (Mu et al., 2014; Raskutti et al., 2019). To circumvent this issue, we exploit an equivalent vectorized representation of model (1): vec( Yk,i ) =  (A0)[SX ] + ( Bk)[SX ]

 vec( Xk,i ) + vec( Ek,i ), (10) where SX = [ d + m] \ [m] denotes the set of modes corresponding to the predictor tensors {Xk,i }. Under this representation, ( A0)[SX ] is of low rank because A0 has low Tucker rank r and rank(( A0)[SX ]) ≤ min( rq , r p)with rq = Qmℓ=1 rℓ and rp = Qdℓ=1 rm+ℓ (see Lemma 4 in Mu et al. (2014)). Meanwhile, ( Bk)[SX ] inherits the weak sparsity of Bk, as matricization preserves sparsity patterns. Consequently, it is natural to regularize the nuclear norm of ( A0)[SX ] and the ℓ1 norm of Bk (i.e., ( Bk)[SX ]). This leads to the preliminary one-stage estimator for client k:( eA0,k , eBk) = argmin A0,k ,Bk

(

12nknkX

> i=1

Yk,i − ⟨ A0,k + Bk, Xk,i ⟩ 2F + λk∥(A0,k )[SX ]∥∗ + ϖk∥Bk∥1

)

, (11) subject to ∥(Bk)[SX ]∥op ≤ ζ, where λk and ϖk are tuning parameters that control the respective regularization strengths. The operator-norm bound imposes a mild weak-identifiability condition that prevents the sparse deviation from spuriously acting as a low-rank component (Agarwal et al., 2012). This convex optimization problem can be solved efficiently via the alternating direction method of multipliers (Neal et al., 2011); see Algorithm 5 in Section S.7.3 of the Supplementary Material for details. For the single-client setting, the eA0,k directly serves as the local initialization, i.e., Aloc ,(0) 0,k = eA0,k . In the federated setting, we initialize the shared component A0 with the estimate from the client having the largest sample size, i.e., A(0) 0 = eA0,k ⋆ with k⋆ = argmax k∈[K]nk.

## 4.2 Tucker-Rank Selection 

The Tucker rank of A0 ∈ Rq1×···× qm×p1×···× pd is r = ( r1, . . . , r d+m) with rs = rank(( A0)[s]) and ( A0)[s] being the mode-s matricization for s ∈ [d + m]. Hence, selecting the Tucker rank of A0 reduces to estimating the ranks of its mode-wise matricizations. We adopt the ridge-type ratio criterion (Xia et al., 2015) to determine these matrix ranks. For the single-client case, the Tucker rank r is selected based on the preliminary one-stage estimator eA0,k 

in Section 4.1. Let eσk,s,r denote the r-th singular value of the mode-s matricization of eA0,k . Using the local 8dataset Dk of client k, we estimate the rank along mode s (i.e. rs) by the following ridge-type ratio criterion 

brk,s = argmin 1≤r≤¯rs−1

eσk,s,r +1 + cs(p, q, n k)

eσk,s,r + cs(p, q, n k) , for s ∈ [d + m], (12) where ¯ rs is a prescribed upper bound for rs, and cs(p, q, n k) is a ridge-type penalty that stabilizes the ratio, with p = ( p1, . . . , p d) and q = ( q1, . . . , q m) for simplicity. Motivated by the consistency conditions for ridge-type ratio estimators in Theorem 5, we recommend setting cs(p, q, n k) = 10 −2ppq/n k, where p = Qdℓ=1 pℓ and 

q = Qmℓ=1 qℓ. The resulting Tucker-rank estimator for client k is given by brloc  

> k

= ( brk, 1, . . . , brk,d +m). In the federated setting, we apply (12) to each preliminary estimator eA0,k , thereby obtaining client-specific candidate ranks {brk,s }Kk=1 for each mode s ∈ [d + m]. To robustify the rank selection against unstable local estimates, we aggregate these candidates by taking the mode across clients: brfed  

> s

= mode( {br1,s , . . . , brK,s }) for

## 4.3 Tuning Parameter Selection 

The proposed federated two-stage procedure needs to choose the tuning parameters including the step size ηA

in Stage-I, and the regularization parameters {ωk}Kk=1 in Stage-II. Before selecting these parameters, we first fix hyper-parameters such as the truncation levels τX,k and τ (t)

> E,k

, the standard deviation σ(t) 

> k

of the Gaussian noise for privacy, the iteration numbers Tg , T (k)

> i=1

for each client k. At global iteration t,we similarly set τ (t) 

> E,k

> i=1

We therefore use the empirical maxima to avoid information loss. To ensure that the Stage-I procedure satisfies the desired ( ε, δ )-DP guarantee, we set the standard deviation of the Gaussian noise as

In our numerical studies, we adopt V = 5 as a practical trade-off between bias, variance, and computational cost (James et al., 2013). # 5 Theoretical Analysis 

We first specify the structural parameter space induced by the decomposition (2). The shared component A∗

> 0

is assumed to have Tucker rank r = ( r1, · · · , r d+m) with all rs fixed and independent of the tensor dimensions. The client-specific sparse deviations satisfy B∗ 

> k

∈ Bν for all k ∈ [K], where, for ν ∈ [0 , 1), 

Bν = B ∈ Rq1×···× qm×p1×···× pd : 1{ν=0 }∥B∥0/s 0 + 1{0<ν< 1}∥B∥νν /s ν ≤ 1 .

Under this sparse condition on the client-specific deviations, we define hB,k = ∥B∗

> k

∥1−ν/ 2 

> ∞

s1/2 

> ν

for k ∈ [K], which bounds the Frobenius norm via ∥B∗

> k

∥F ≤ hB,k for each client k. The largest deviation bound across clients is denoted by ¯hB = max k∈[K] hB,k .The decomposition (2) is not automatically identifiable, because a sparse tensor may also have low-rank structure, and conversely a low-rank tensor may contain sparse patterns. Motivated by the equivalent vectorized representation (10), we introduce the following weak-identifiability condition to resolve this ambiguity. 

Assumption 1 (Weak identifiability) . There exists a constant ζ > 0 such that 

max  

> k∈[K]

∥(B∗

> k

)[SX ]∥op ≤ ζ. 

Assumption 1 restricts the sparse deviation from displaying a spuriously strong low-rank component along the matricization; see Agarwal et al. (2012) for analogous discussions. Next, we impose regularity conditions on the covariates and noise tensors. These assumptions underpin the non-asymptotic error bounds developed in the subsequent analysis. 

Assumption 2 (Sub-Gaussian design) . The collections {Xk,i }nk 

> i=1

are independent across clients k ∈ [K], and for each client the observations {Xk,i }nk 

> i=1

are i.i.d. across i. Moreover, the vectorized design tensor admits the representation vec( Xk,i ) = Σ1/2

> X,k

ξk,i , where ΣX,k ∈ Rp×p with p = Qdℓ=1 pℓ, and ξk,i ∈ Rp has i.i.d. standardized sub-Gaussian entries with parameter σX,k . That is, E(ξk,i ) = 0, Cov( ξk,i ) = Ip, and for every j ∈ [p] and μ ∈ R,

E exp( μξ k,i,j ) ≤ exp( μ2σ2

> X,k

/2) .

Assumption 3 (Conditionally sub-Gaussian noise) . The collections {Ek,i }nk 

> i=1

are independent across clients 

k ∈ [K], and for each client the noise tensors {Ek,i }nk 

> i=1

are i.i.d. across i. Conditional on Xk,i , the random vector vec( Ek,i ) is mean-zero and sub-Gaussian with covariance proxy ΣE,k and parameter σE,k . That is, 

10 E[vec( Ek,i ) | Xk,i ] = 0 and, for any u ∈ Rq with q = Qmℓ=1 qℓ and μ ∈ R, E [exp ( μ⟨u, vec( Ek,i )⟩) | Xk,i ] ≤

exp( μ2σ2

> E,k

u⊤ΣE,k u/2) , where ΣE,k ∈ Rq×q .

Assumption 2 requires the design tensor Xk,i to satisfy a sub-Gaussian condition with client-specific covari-ance structure, a standard requirement in high-dimensional regression analysis (Negahban et al., 2009; Tony Cai and Guo, 2020). Assumption 3 further assumes that the noise tensor Ek,i is conditionally mean-zero and condi-tionally sub-Gaussian given the design tensor. Notably, this is weaker than the assumption of full independence between the design and noise adopted in many high-dimensional regression settings (Negahban et al., 2009; Tony Cai and Guo, 2020). 

## 5.1 Theory for Stage-I Representation Learning 

We begin by recalling the standard notion of differential privacy, which quantifies the information leakage of the federated Stage-I procedure. Two local datasets Dk = {(Xk,i , Yk,i )}nk 

> i=1

and D′ 

> k

= {(X′

> k,i

, Y′

> k,i

)}nk 

> i=1

are called 

adjacent if they differ in exactly one observation pair. 

Definition 1 (( ε, δ )-differential privacy (Dwork et al., 2006)) . A randomized mechanism M satisfies (ε, δ )-differential privacy if, for any two adjacent datasets Dk and D′ 

> k

and any measurable set O in the output space of M,

P(M(Dk) ∈ O ) ≤ exp( ε)P(M(D′

> k

) ∈ O ) + δ. 

In Definition 1, the datasets Dk and D′ 

> k

are treated as fixed inputs, and the probabilities are taken only over the randomness of the mechanism M (Lu et al., 2026). In our setting, this randomness stems exclusively from the Gaussian perturbations added during the federated updates, rather than from the sampling randomness of the observed data. We now present the theoretical guarantees for Stage-I representation learning in Theorems 1 and 2. To simplify the exposition, we collect the relevant model and algorithmic quantities that will appear throughout the analysis. For each client k, let λmin  

> X,k

and λmax  

> X,k

denote the minimum and maximum eigenvalues of ΣX,k ,respectively, and let λmax  

> E,k

denote the maximum eigenvalue of ΣE,k . The corresponding global envelope quantities are defined as λmin

= min k∈[K] λmin  

> X,k

and ¯λmax  

> X

= max k∈[K] λmax  

> X,k

. We further introduce ¯ κX = ¯λmax  

> X

> X

= max k∈[K] λmax  

> E,k

On the algorithmic side, recall that τX,k and τ (t) 

> E,k

We now interpret the conditions and the error bound in Theorem 1. The Gaussian noise level σ(t) 

> k

is chosen to ensure that each transmitted gradient satisfies the Gaussian-mechanism privacy guarantee, and composition over Tg iterations yields the claimed ( ε, δ )-differential privacy for each client. The Stage-I step-size condition 

ηA ∈ I fed  

> A

and the upper bound on the federated design condition number ¯ κX guarantee sufficient local curvature of the population loss around A∗ 

> 0

and contractivity of the Riemannian gradient update. The initialization requirement ∥A(0) 0 − A∗

> 0

∥F ≤ RA places the initial iterate inside this region of contraction. The upper bound on ζ controls the bias induced by the non-identifiable sparse deviations, preventing them from dominating the low-rank update and pushing the iterates outside the contraction region. The pooled sample-size condition has two parts: the term Mfed  

> A

df r is the standard requirement in high-dimensional tensor regression, reflecting that the low Tucker-rank structure reduces the effective complexity from the ambient tensor dimension to the degrees of freedom df r . The remainder ensures that the privacy perturbations accumulated over Tg iterations are sufficiently small for every noisy Stage-I iterate to remain within the contraction region. Under these conditions, Theorem 1 establishes the estimation guarantee for the federated Stage-I estimator. The resulting error bound has three components. The first term is the statistical error for estimating the shared low Tucker-rank component from the pooled sample. The second term √rq + rp ζ is the unavoidable cost of weak identifiability: without a strict separation between low-rank and sparse structures, a bias of this order is generally unavoidable, as confirmed by the minimax lower bound in Theorem 6. The third term is the privacy cost induced by the Gaussian perturbations injected into the transmitted gradients. This term scales as 1 /ε with respect to the privacy budget ε and only as plog(log n/δ ) with respect to the failure probability δ, reflecting the standard privacy–utility trade-off with a mild logarithmic dependence on 1 /δ . Plugging in the theoretical orders of τX,k and τ (t)

> E,k

, the privacy cost is, up to logarithmic factors and model-dependent constants, of order 

√Kdf r (p + √pq )/(εn ). Therefore, the sample size required to make the privacy overhead negligible needs only to exceed the dimension-reduced quantity ε−1√Kdf r(p + √pq ), rather than the full ambient dimension pq . This demonstrates that privacy can be achieved at a modest sample-size premium. Before stating the single-client analogue, we introduce the corresponding client-specific notation. The same rank and signal-strength symbols rq , rp, df r , and μmin  

> A

are reused. For a fixed client k, let κX,k = λmax  

> X,k

/λ min 

> X,k

denote the client-specific design condition number. The admissible Stage-I step-size interval IA,k , the initial-ization radius RA,k , and the sample-size constant Msingle  

> A,k

are collected in Section S.1.2 of the Supplementary 12 Material for readability.

Theorem 2 provides the corresponding Stage-I guarantee when estimation is performed solely on the local data of client k. The conditions on the design condition number κX,k , step size ηA,k , initialization radius 

RA,k , and weak-identifiability level ζ play exactly the same roles as their federated counterparts, now expressed in client-specific form. Likewise, the requirement on the local sample size nk is the single-client analogue of the pooled sample size condition in Theorem 1. Since the single-client procedure does not communicate any information across clients, it requires no privacy perturbation. Consequently, the error bound contains only the local statistical error (scaled by nk) and the same weak-identifiability cost √rq + rp ζ, with no privacy term.

The minimax lower bound in Theorem 6 shows that both the federated and single-client personalized error bounds are rate-optimal in their respective regimes. Hence, a direct comparison of the two bounds provides a fair way to evaluate the benefit of federation. The federated procedure improves the Stage-I statistical term by replacing the local sample size nk with the pooled size n, but it also incurs an additional privacy cost. When the client-specific quantities hB,k , σX,k ,

σE,k , and the eigenvalue bounds are of the same order across clients, the federated bound enjoys a smaller dominant statistical error. Thus, the federated estimator is preferable when this pooled-sample gain dominates the privacy cost. A sufficient condition for this to hold for client k is

This condition is mild in high-dimensional settings and transparently captures the privacy-utility trade-off. A smaller privacy budget ε (stronger privacy) inflates the required pooled sample size as 1 /ε , while the failure probability δ exerts only a logarithmic influence through plog(log n/δ ).

Theorem 4 characterizes the statistical accuracy for the optimizer of convex problem in (11), which serves as the preliminary estimator for initializing the two-stage procedures. Theorem 5 provides a consistency guarantee for the Tucker-rank selection step based on the preliminary estimator eA0,k . The lower bound on the ridge penalty cs(p, q, n k) ensures that the estimation error from Theorem 4 is dominated by the ridge term, which stabilizes the spurious small singular values beyond the true rank. The upper bound requires the ridge penalty to be smaller than the signal gap among the nonzero singular values of ( A∗

> 0

)[s], so that the true rank location is not masked. Under this separation, the ridge-type ratio criterion in (12) consistently selects the true Tucker rank r∗ with probability tending to one. 

## 5.4 Minimax Lower Bounds 

We finally establish minimax lower bounds for both the federated and single-client regimes under model (1) and decomposition (2). To compare these two regimes on a common footing, we specialize to the isotropic Gaussian submodel with vec( Xk,i ) ∼ N (0, Ip) and vec( Ek,i ) ∼ N (0, Iq ), independent across clients and samples. 15 Theorem 6 shows that, excluding the additional error induced by a finite privacy budget, the upper bounds derived above are rate-optimal under the current minimax framework. Specifically, the low Tucker-rank term of the lower bound scales as pdf r /n in the federated regime and pdf r /n k in the single-client regime, matching the corresponding terms in the upper bounds up to constants that depend on the design and noise parameters. The sparse term sν,k also coincides with the local sparse estimation rate obtained in the upper bounds. Finally, the weak-identifiability term in the lower bound is √rq ∧ rp ζ, whereas the upper bounds contain √rq + rp ζ. Since each mode rank rs is fixed and does not grow with the tensor dimensions, both rq and rp are finite constants, and hence these two terms are of the same order. 

# 6 Simulation Studies 

We conduct simulation studies to evaluate the finite-sample performance of the proposed personalized feder-ated tensor regression method. We first compare its estimation accuracy with several benchmark methods across varying per-client sample sizes, and then examine the privacy–utility trade-off under different privacy parameters. In all experiments, the federated and single-client algorithms are initialized using the procedure in Section 4.1, the Tucker rank is selected according to Section 4.2, and remaining tuning parameters are cho-sen as described in Section 4.3. ADMM-based initialization tuning follows Section S.7.4 of the Supplementary Material. We consider K = 5 clients, with coefficient tensors Ak = A0 + Bk ∈ R5×5×10 ×5. The generation of the common and client-specific components proceeds as follows. The shared component A0 has Tucker rank 

r = (3 , 3, 3, 3). We first draw a core tensor G ∈ R3×3×3×3 with i.i.d. standard normal entries and rescale it to satisfy ∥G∥F = 1. We then independently generate random Gaussian matrices of sizes 5 × 3, 5 × 3, 10 × 3, and 5 × 3, and orthonormalize their columns to obtain factor matrices Us such that U⊤ 

> s

Us = Irs for s ∈ [4]. The shared component is formed as A0 = G ×4 

> s=1

Us. For each client k, the sparse deviation Bk is constructed by selecting 50 entries uniformly at random, drawing their values independently from N(0 , 1), and setting all remaining entries to zero. The resulting tensor is then rescaled so that ∥Bk∥F/∥A0∥F = 0 .5. Given the true coefficient tensors Ak = A0 + Bk, we generate the predictor and response tensors {(Xk,i , Yk,i )} from model (1). The entries of Xk,i and the noise tensor Ek,i are i.i.d. from N(0 , 1) and N(0 , 0.01), respectively. The response 

Yk,i is then computed according to (1). To evaluate estimation accuracy, we compute the Frobenius-norm error of the shared component as 

∥A0 − A0∥F, and the average errors for the client-specific deviations and personalized coefficient tensors as 5−1 P5 

> k=1

∥Bk − Bk∥F and 5 −1 P5 

> k=1

∥Ak − Ak∥F, respectively. Here A0, Bk, and Ak denote the correspond-ing estimators. We compare four estimation strategies: (i) the non-private personalized federated estimator with ( ε, δ ) = ( ∞, 1), (ii) the private personalized federated estimator with ( ε, δ ) = (20 , 0.1), (iii) the single-client two-stage estimator, and (iv) the ADMM initialization obtained by averaging the five client-specific initial estimators. Figure 1 displays the estimation errors for A0, {Bk}5

> k=1

> k=1

via boxplots over 1,000 replications at different per-client sample sizes. For all methods, the errors decrease as the per-client sample size grows, corroborating the theoretical results. At each sample size, the non-private federated estimator has the smallest error, followed by the private federated estimator, the single-client two-stage estimator, and the ADMM ini-tialization. The gap between the two federated estimators reflects the cost of privacy protection, while their improvement over the single-client estimator demonstrates the benefit of cross-client information sharing. The 16 advantage of the single-client two-stage estimator over the ADMM initialization further highlights the value of the two-stage refinement. Moreover, the gap between the private and non-private federated estimators shrinks as the sample size increases, indicating that the effect of the added Gaussian noise becomes less pronounced as more observations become available at each client. 50 100 150 200 250

# 7 Empirical Analysis 

ADHD symptoms are clinically meaningful and important for early identification, monitoring, and individual-ized intervention. Empirical evidence shows that structural MRI features are associated with ADHD symptoms (Zhou et al., 2013). This section therefore investigates the role of MRI in analyzing and predicting ADHD symptom severity. Because the participating sites collect comparable imaging and clinical variables, borrowing 17 information across sites may improve analysis and prediction. However, privacy and data-sharing restrictions prevent direct pooling of individual-level records. Moreover, MRI information is typically recorded as a ten-sor. These considerations make our DP federated learning framework of tensor regression well suited for this application. We use data from three sites participating in the ADHD-200 Sample Initiative: the Kennedy Krieger In-stitute (KKI), Peking University (Peking), and New York University (NYU). The data are publicly available at http://fcon_1000.projects.nitrc.org/indi/adhd200/ . After excluding subjects with missing or invalid measurements, the final sample comprises 556 subjects: 80 from KKI, 221 from Peking, and 255 from NYU. Following the Daubechies D4 wavelet-based image-reduction procedure used in Hou and Chaib-draa (2015), we transform each preprocessed structural MRI scan into a 12 × 14 × 12 tensor and denote the resulting covariate for subject i at site k by Xk,i ∈ R12 ×14 ×12 . Let Yk,i ∈ R denote the ADHD symptom score, a quantitative index in the dataset that measures ADHD symptom severity. We consider the scalar-on-tensor regression 

Yk,i = ⟨Ak, Xk,i ⟩ + Ek,i , with k ∈ { KKI , NYU , Peking }, (14) where Ek,i ∈ R is mean-zero random noise and Ak ∈ R12 ×14 ×12 is the site-specific coefficient tensor. Because structural MRI measurements exhibit strong spatial dependence and common neuroanatomical organization, the imaging-symptom association shared across sites is expected to admit a low-dimensional Tucker-rank structure. In contrast, differences in scanners, acquisition protocols, or subject populations are expected to affect only a limited subset of imaging features, motivating sparse site-specific deviations. Therefore, we decompose Ak into a shared low Tucker-rank component A0 and a weakly sparse site-specific deviation Bk as in (2). To implement the proposed federated procedure in Section 3.1, we first compute the local one-stage estima-tors in Section 4.1 and use the preliminary estimate from NYU, the site with the largest sample size, to initialize Stage-I. Applying the ridge-type ratio criterion in Section 4.2 to the preliminary estimators eA0,k and aggre-gating the site-specific rank candidates yields the federated Tucker-rank estimate brfed = (1 , 3, 1). We set the privacy parameters to ( ε, δ ) = (20 , 0.1). Following Section 4.3, the remaining tuning parameters are selected by five-fold cross-validation, and the iteration numbers are set according to the implementation guidelines therein. Figure 3 visualizes the mode-3 unfoldings of the estimated shared component bA0 and the three site-specific deviations { bBk}. Consistent with the structural decomposition in (2), bA0 exhibits low Tucker-rank structure and captures the imaging–symptom association shared across sites through a few multilinear spatial factors. By projecting the high-magnitude entries of bA0 back to the standard brain space, we identify approximate correspondence with the left middle and superior frontal gyri, cerebellar Crus regions, temporal/fusiform areas, and the left temporal pole. These frontal, cerebellar, and temporal regions are well-documented in the ADHD neuroimaging literature as sites of structural alteration (Castellanos et al., 1996; Berquin et al., 1998; Kobel et al., 2010). In contrast, the nonzero coefficients of each bBk constitute localized adjustments to the shared pattern, allowing the model to flexibly absorb site-specific effects induced by differences in acquisition protocols or scanner characteristics. We next evaluate the prediction of ADHD symptom score under model (14) by comparing the pro-posed personalized federated two-stage method ( Fed-2Stage ) with three benchmarks: (i) federated averaging method ( Fed-Avg ), which aggregates the local estimates by simple averaging, and uses the resulting tensor

The proposed method Fed-2Stage achieves the smallest site-specific validation error at all three sites and the smallest equal-weight aggregate validation error. Its aggregate validation error is 0.805, representing reductions of 23.9%, 42.3%, and 27.5% relative to Fed-Avg , Fed-Common , and Single-2Stage , respectively. The comparison with 

Fed-Avg further suggests that jointly estimating a shared low Tucker-rank component and sparse site-specific deviations is more effective than simply averaging independently fitted local coefficients. The improvement over Fed-Common indicates that a common federated component alone does not adequately account for site heterogeneity, while the improvement over Single-2Stage demonstrates the benefit of borrowing information across sites. In addition, Fed-2Stage has the smallest fold-to-fold standard deviations for NYU and the equal-weight aggregate measure and the second smallest for KKI, indicating generally stable predictive performance. Overall, these results support combining differentially private cross-site estimation of the shared structure with local personalization under ( ε, δ ) = (20 , 0.1). 19 Table 1: Five-fold site-specific and equal-weight aggregate validation errors for four methods, with fold-to-fold standard deviations in parentheses. # 8 Conclusion and Discussion 

This paper develops a privacy-preserving personalized federated learning framework for high-dimensional tensor regression. The proposed method decomposes each client-specific coefficient tensor into a low Tucker-rank component shared across clients and a sparse client-specific deviation, thereby capturing common multilinear structure while accommodating client heterogeneity. Estimation proceeds in two stages: differentially private federated representation learning for the shared component through noisy gradient aggregation, followed by local personalized estimation of the client-specific deviations. We establish non-asymptotic error bounds and matching minimax lower bounds for both the federated and single-client estimators, together with theoretical guarantees for the initialization estimator and Tucker-rank selection procedure. A direct comparison of the federated and single-client rates reveals that federated learning improves upon single-client estimation when the reduction in statistical error from cross-client information sharing outweighs the additional cost of privacy protection. Simulation studies corroborate the theoretical findings and demonstrate the advantages of the proposed method across varying levels of local sample sizes and privacy budgets. An empirical analysis of ADHD symptom index further shows that the federated method achieves desirable predictive performance while respecting privacy constraints, highlighting its practical value for privacy-sensitive and heterogeneous tensor data. Several promising directions remain for future research. First, the proposed framework can be extended to accommodate mixed covariates of different tensor orders, including scalars, vectors, matrices, and higher-order tensors. This would allow the inclusion of standard control variables and demographic factors, thereby yielding a more comprehensive characterization of the association with the response. Second, it is of interest to generalize the methodology to settings with dependent data. Both the estimation procedures and the theoretical guarantees should be adapted to handle temporal or spatial dependence, which is common in longitudinal and spatially organized neuroimaging studies. Third, the current linear specification can be extended to nonlinear tensor regression models, such as additive, kernel-based, and neural network models. Such extensions would capture more flexible relationships between tensor-valued covariates and the response, broadening the scope of potential applications. 

# Appendix 

This supplementary material provides supporting technical details for the main theoretical and algorithmic results. Section S.1 introduces the notation and theoretical constants used throughout the main paper and the supplement. Section S.2 collects the auxiliary results required for proving the main theorems. Section S.3 establishes the upper bounds for the federated and single-client procedures, as well as the consistency of Tucker-20 rank selection. Section S.4 proves the corresponding minimax lower bounds. Section S.5 gives the detailed proofs of the auxiliary lemmas. Section S.6 presents additional technical tools used in the theoretical analysis. Finally, Section S.7 describes the implementation of the proposed estimation procedures and the tuning-parameter selection strategy.

Lemma B.13 (Tucker rank of tangent-space elements) . Let A ∈ Rq1×···× qm×p1×···× pd be a Tucker-rank-r

tensor. Then, for any T ∈ T r (A), Tucrank( T) ≤ 2r.

# S.3 Proofs of Upper Bounds and Consistency Results 

We organize this section as follows. We first prove the single-client Stage-I representation error bound in Theorem 2 and then extend the same arguments to the federated setting in Theorem 1. Next, we prove the personalized estimation error bound in Theorem 3, which refines the shared representation estimator by 24 estimating the client-specific sparse deviation. Finally, we establish the one-stage initialization error bound in Theorem 4 and the Tucker-rank selection consistency result in Theorem 5. 

Proof of Theorem 2 . We organize the proof into four steps. Step 1 introduces the deterministic notation used throughout the arguments. Step 2 defines the high probability event on which all empirical processes are controlled uniformly over deterministic rank classes. Conditional on these events, Step 3 derives the one-step recursion directly for the t-th projected-gradient iterate. Step 4 uses this recursion to derive the final error bound.

Proof of Theorem 1 . The proof is similar to that of Theorem 2, but with the local empirical gradient replaced by the weighted federated gradient and with an additional Gaussian privacy-noise term. We organize the proof into five steps. Step 1 introduces the deterministic notation and auxiliary constants used throughout the argument. Step 2 derives the sensitivity of the truncated local gradient. Step 3 defines the high probability event on which the pooled empirical processes, truncation event, and aggregated privacy noise are controlled uniformly over deterministic rank classes. Conditional on this event, Step 4 derives the one-step recursion for the federated projected-gradient iterate. Step 5 verifies the privacy guarantee and uses the recursion to establish the final error bound.

Let Vt = {vec( Z) : Z ∈ T r (A(t)0 )} be the vectorized tangent space. Since vec( ·) is a linear bijection from the ambient tensor space to its vectorized Euclidean space, it preserves dimension. Thus rt = dim( Vt) = dim  Tr (A(t)0 ) ≤ df r . Let qt, 1, · · · , qt,r t be an orthonormal basis of Vt, and define Qt = ( qt, 1, · · · , qt,r t ) ∈

R(Qmj=1 qj ×Qdj=1 pj )×rt . Then Q⊤ 

> t

Qt = Irt and span( Qt) = Vt. Since the columns of Qt form an orthonormal basis of Vt, the Euclidean orthogonal projection of any vector vec( Z) onto Vt is Ptvec( Z), where Pt = QtQ⊤

> t

is the orthogonal projection matrix onto an rt-dimensional subspace. Consequently, Pt is symmetric and idempotent, i.e., P⊤ 

> t

= Pt and P2 

> t

= Pt. Its eigenvalues are either 0 or 1, and therefore rank( Pt) = rt ≤ df r ,

∥Pt∥op ≤ 1, and ∥Pt∥2F = rank( Pt) = rt ≤ df r .34 On the other hand, PTr (A(t)0 )(Z) is the orthogonal projection of Z onto the tangent space of A(t)0 . Since vectorization only stacks the entries of a tensor into a vector without changing inner products, Frobenius orthog-onality in the tensor space is equivalent to Euclidean orthogonality after vectorization. Therefore, vectorizing the Frobenius-orthogonal projection of Z onto Tr (A(t)0 ) is the same as first vectorizing Z and then taking the Euclidean orthogonal projection onto Vt, i.e., for every tensor Z, vec( PTr (A(t)0 )(Z)) = Ptvec( Z). is ( ε/T g , δ/T g )-DP for client k at iteration t. The subsequent server-side aggregation, tangent-space projection, and update are post-processing operations that do not access the raw local data and therefore incur no additional privacy loss. By the basic composition theorem, e.g., Dwork and Roth (2014, 39 Theorem 3.1.6), the sequence of messages released by client k over Tg iterations is ( ε, δ )-DP. # S.4 Proof of Minimax Lower Bounds 

Proof of Theorem 6 . The proof for Theorem 6 follows by deriving Frobenius-norm lower bounds for esti-mating A∗ 

> 0

and {B∗

> k

}Kk=1 in three complementary cases. The first concerns structural non-identifiability, as established in Theorem C.1; the second concerns estimation of the low Tucker-rank shared component A∗

> 0

in the single-client or federated settings, as established in Theorems C.2 or C.3; and the third concerns es-timation of the sparse client-specific deviations {B∗

> k

}Kk=1 , as established in Theorem C.4. For the statistical lower bounds, we restrict attention to the normalized Gaussian submodel in which vec( Xk,i ) ∼ N (0, Ip) and vec( Ek,i ) ∼ N (0, Iq ) independently across clients and observations and independently of one another. This submodel satisfies Assumptions 2 and 3; hence, a minimax lower bound established on it also applies to the full model class. Combining the lower bounds obtained in these three cases yields the desired joint minimax lower bound.

Construction 2: factor-space variation. This construction follows the same idea as the factor-space packing arguments in the proof of Luo and Zhang (2024, Theorem 4). Let S ∈ Rr1×···× rd+m be a fixed full Tucker-rank core tensor such that, for each mode s ∈ [d + m], 

cα ≤ σrs (S[s]) ≤ σ1(S[s]) ≤ Cα,

Next, we construct an ε-net to deduce the uniform bound over the Tucker-rank class Sr from the pointwise concentration bound. Let Ncore be an ε-net of the Frobenius unit ball in Rr1×···× rd+m , and, for each mode s ∈ [d + m], let Ns be an 

ε-net of the corresponding Stiefel manifold with respect to the projection distance d(U, V) = ∥UU ⊤ −VV ⊤∥op .By Lemma 7 in Zhang and Xia (2018), for any 0 < ε < 1, we can construct an ε-net Ncore = {S(1) , · · · , S(NS )}

for the Frobenius unit ball {S ∈ Rr1×···× rd+m : ∥S∥F ≤ 1} such that sup S:∥S∥F≤1 min 1≤i≤NS ∥S − S(i)∥F ≤ ε,with |N core | ≤ (3 /ε )Qd+ms=1 rs .At the same time, for each s ∈ [d + m], define the ambient mode dimension ds = qs for s ∈ [m], and ds = ps−m for s ∈ [d + m] \ [m]. By Proposition 8 in Szarek (1982), for each s ∈ [d + m], we can construct an ε-net Ns = {U(1)  

> s

> s

Proof of Lemma B.13 . Write A = S ×d+ma=1 Ua, where Ua ∈ Oda,r a with da = qa for a ∈ [m] and da = pa−m

for a ∈ [d + m] \ [m]. By the tangent-space characterization of the Tucker-rank manifold as in Luo and Zhang (2024), any T ∈ T r (A) can be written as 

T = F ×d+ma=1 Ua +

> d+m

X

> j=1

S ×j Uj, ⊥Dj ×a̸=j Ua,

where F ∈ Rr1×···× rd+m and Dj ∈ R(dj −rj )×rj . Fix a mode ℓ ∈ [d + m]. In the mode-ℓ matricization, 

F ×d+ma=1 Ua and S ×j Uj, ⊥Dj ×a̸=j Ua with j̸ = ℓ have their mode-ℓ column spaces contained in span( Uℓ). 

S ×ℓ Uℓ, ⊥Dℓ ×a̸=ℓ Ua has mode-ℓ column space contained in span( Uℓ, ⊥Dℓ), whose dimension is at most rℓ.79 Therefore col( T[ℓ]) ⊆ span( Uℓ) + span( Uℓ, ⊥Dℓ),

and hence rank( T[ℓ]) ≤ 2rℓ. Since this holds for ℓ ∈ [d + m], we have Tucrank( T) ≤ 2r.

Lemma E.3 (Luo and Zhang, 2024, Quasi-projection property of T-HOSVD and ST-HOSVD (Proposition 3)) . Let Mr denote the set of tensors with Tucker rank at most r = ( r1, · · · , r d+m). ## S.7.2 T-HOSVD and ST-HOSVD 

Given an mode-ℓ tensor Y ∈ Rp1×···× pℓ and a target Tucker rank r = ( r1, · · · , r ℓ), a common retraction onto the low Tucker-rank manifold is based on truncated high-order singular value decomposition (T-HOSVD) or sequentially truncated high-order singular value decomposition (ST-HOSVD).

## S.7.4 Tuning Parameter Selection for Single-Client Estimation 

For the two-stage single-client procedure, we use the same validation-based principle as the federated two-stage procedure to select the tuning parameters.For a fixed client k, we randomly split its local dataset Dk into a training set Dtr  

> k

and a validation set Dval  

> k

. After fixing the Tucker rank, the number of Stage I and Stage II iterations, the retraction method, and the Stage II stepsize ηD,k , the remaining tuning parameters are the Stage I stepsize ηA,k and the Stage II regularization parameter ωk.For each candidate pair ( ηA,k , ω k) on a prescribed grid, all model fitting steps are carried out using only the training set Dtr  

> k

. We first run the local Stage I procedure to obtain the low-rank estimator bAloc 

> 0,k

(ηA,k ), and then run the local Stage II personalization step to obtain the sparse deviation estimator bBloc k ( bAloc 

> 0,k

(ηA,k ), ω k). 84 Each candidate pair is evaluated by the validation squared Frobenius prediction error Error loc  

> val ,k

= 1

|Dval

> k

> 0,k

(ηA,k ) + bBloc k

  bAloc

(ηA,k ), ω k

, Xk,i 

E 2F

.

The selected tuning parameters are the candidate pair that minimizes Error loc

over the prescribed grid.
